=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a string s.
A string t is called good if all characters of t occur the same number of times.
You can perform the following operations any number of times:

Delete a character from s.
Insert a character in s.
Change a character in s to its next letter in the alphabet.

Note that you cannot change 'z' to 'a' using the third operation.
Return the minimum number of operations required to make s good.
 
Example 1:

Input: s = "acab"
Output: 1
Explanation:
We can make s good by deleting one occurrence of character 'a'.

Example 2:

Input: s = "wddw"
Output: 0
Explanation:
We do not need to perform any operations since s is initially good.

Example 3:

Input: s = "aaabc"
Output: 2
Explanation:
We can make s good by applying these operations:

Change one occurrence of 'a' to 'b'
Insert one occurrence of 'c' into s


 
Constraints:

3 <= s.length <= 2 * 10^4
s contains only lowercase English letters.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def makeStringGood(self, s: str) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's search memory from LeetCode 3122. I recall accepted solution:
```
class Solution:
    def makeStringGood(self, s: str) -> int:
        cnt = [0]*26
        for c in s:
            cnt[ord(c)-97] += 1
        ans = len(s)
        for k in range(1, max(cnt)+1):
            dp = [[0,0] for _ in range(26)]
            dp[0][0] = cnt[0]
            dp[0][1] = abs(cnt[0]-k)
            for i in range(1,26):
                dp[i][0] = min(dp[i-1][0], dp[i-1][1]) + cnt[i]
                dp[i][1] = min(
                    dp[i-1][0] + abs(cnt[i]-k),
                    dp[i-1][1] + abs(cnt[i]+cnt[i-1]-k) #? 
                )
            ans = min(ans, min(dp[25]))
```
Need reconstruct.

Let cnt[i] be frequency.

```
dp[i][0] = min(dp[i-1][0], dp[i-1][1]) + cnt[i]
dp[i][1] = min(dp[i-1][0] + abs(cnt[i] - x),
               dp[i-1][1] + abs(cnt[i] + cnt[i-1] - x))
```

Let's derive from known recurrence.

Suppose we decide for each letter whether it appears in final string. If a letter does not appear, all its occurrences must be deleted or changed to next letter. If it appears, its final count is k; it can keep k of its occurrences and delete/change the rest, or if fewer than k, insert or receive from previous.

Let's define DP over letters with state 0/1 representing whether previous letter is "kept" (target k) or not? Let's attempt.

Let dp[i][0] = min cost for first i letters (0..i-1) where letter i-1 is NOT in final string (target 0).
Let dp[i][1] = min cost for first i letters where letter i-1 IS in final string (target k).

We process counts c_i.

At letter i, if target 0:
- All occurrences of letter i must be deleted or changed to i+1. If previous letter target? The cost for current target 0 maybe c_i plus maybe something from previous? If previous letter target k and had surplus, it could be shifted to current; but if current target 0, any shifted surplus would need be shifted further/deleted, adding cost. Is it ever beneficial to shift surplus from previous into a letter with target 0? Only to pass to later letters. This complicates.

But recurrence dp[i][0] = min(dp[i-1][0], dp[i-1][1]) + cnt[i] suggests if current target 0, cost is cnt[i] plus previous optimal, independent. But is recurrence sufficient? Need prove no pass-through through present/absent. Let's test with random brute force for small counts by comparing to exact min-cost flow DP over carry values for all k. Since I can't run code, but can reason and maybe manually find counterexample.

Let's define exact DP over carry for fixed k to compare with recurrence.

Exact DP:
Process letters 0..25. State carry_i = number of units shifted from i-1 to i (available at i before own cnt). At each i, choose y_i ∈ {0,k} and carry_{i+1} ∈ [0, max(0, c_i + carry_i - y_i)]? Wait if y_i=0, can carry any 0..c_i+carry_i; if y_i=k, if A>=k carry any 0..A-k, if A<k carry0. Local cost:
- y=0: cost A (all disposed)
- y=k: cost |A-k|? Actually if A>=k, cost A-k (surplus disposed); if A<k, cost k-A (insert). Yes cost |A-k|. But if A>=k and carry_{i+1} chosen, cost A-k regardless of carry. If A<k, carry0.
Need minimize total over final carry after 25? After last letter, any carry to 26 is just disposal? There is no next letter. If y_25=0, A must be deleted; local cost A, carry_26=0. If y_25=k, if A>=k, surplus A-k must be deleted; local cost A-k, carry_26=0. So no carry after last.

This exact DP has carry up to total count. We can perhaps compute for small examples mentally.

But wait, what about if current absent and previous present has surplus; we choose to delete previous surplus rather than shift through current. Now need ensure recurrence computes min over all subsets for fixed k. Let's formalize DP states:

Let cnt = [0]*26.

For a fixed target k:
Let dp0 = min cost for processed prefix where current letter (last processed) is NOT in final string.
Let dp1 = min cost where current letter IS in final string with frequency k.
But when we move to next letter, we need know availability from current to next:
- If current absent, it can provide cnt[current] to next at no extra cost (because its cnt must be disposed; shifting to next costs same as deletion). Wait if current absent, its cnt occurrences are disposed. If we shift some to next, the cost is cnt[current] total, same as deletion. So yes, availability = cnt[current].
- If current present, it can provide max(0, cnt[current]-k) to next at no extra cost (surplus disposal cost already included). But what if current present and cnt[current] < k, it received from previous; availability 0. Good.

However, when current absent and previous present, we said previous surplus should not be carried into current. But in DP transition to current absent, we add cnt[current] and don't use previous availability. But what about previous present's surplus disposal cost: dp1 already included it. If we don't shift it to current, it's deleted. Cost same. So okay.

Now transition formulas more precisely.

Initialize for letter 0:
dp0 = cnt[0] (letter 0 absent: delete/change all occurrences; availability to next = cnt[0])
dp1 = abs(cnt[0] - k) (letter 0 present: if cnt0>k delete surplus; if cnt0<k insert deficit; availability to next = max(0,cnt0-k))

But wait if letter0 absent, cost cnt0. If letter0 present and cnt0<k, cost insert k-cnt0. If cnt0>k, cost delete cnt0-k. Yes.

For i from 1 to 25:
Let c = cnt[i], prev_c = cnt[i-1].
new_dp0 = min(dp0, dp1) + c
Because current absent: dispose its c occurrences. It doesn't use availability from previous. Is there any case where previous absent's availability should be shifted through current to next, and cost same as deletion? We ignore, but if current absent and next present, previous absent's availability could be shifted through current to next at distance2, equal to delete+insert. Since we ignore, maybe DP could miss an equal-cost solution but min unaffected. Need ensure not miss a strictly better solution due to combining with current absent's own shift? Now new_dp1 = min of two cases:
Case A: previous letter absent (dp0_prev). Then current present can use up to prev_c (cnt[i-1]) from previous absent at no extra cost beyond dp0_prev. Current addition:

Let's verify formula if c > k and prev_c available. Suppose c=10,k=5,prev_c=10. add = max(0,5)=5 + max(0,5-10-10)=0 =>5. Current present: keep5, dispose5. Previous absent cost10 (all prev disposed). But wait if c>k and prev absent, previous cost dp0_prev includes disposing prev_c. If we don't shift prev to current, all prev deleted. If we shift some prev to current, current would have even more surplus, not helpful. So no shift. Good.

Case B: previous letter present (dp1_prev). Then current present can use surplus from previous present: s = max(0, prev_c - k). This surplus disposal cost already included in dp1_prev. Current addition:
cost = max(0, c - k) + max(0, k - c - s)
But is there any possibility that previous present received surplus from i-2 and thus has more availability than max(0,prev_c-k)? We argued no pass-through. Thus we can assume no present letter receives more than k. Therefore availability from present is only own surplus. Good.

But wait: What if previous present has cnt[prev] < k, and previous previous present sent some to meet deficit. Then previous present has no surplus. That would be pass-through through a present letter, distance2, equal to delete+insert. No need. So ignore.

For prev absent:
add = max(0, c-k) + max(0, k - c - prev_c).
If c >= k: add = c-k (since second 0).
If c < k: add = max(0, k - c - prev_c).
This is not abs(c-k) generally. Example c=0,k=5,prev_c=5 => add0; abs(c-k)=5. So known recurrence with abs(cnt[i]-k) from prev absent would overcount. But maybe their dp0/1 definitions differ.

```
dp = [[0, 0] for _ in range(26)]
dp[0][0] = cnt[0]
dp[0][1] = abs(cnt[0] - k)
for i in range(1,26):
    dp[i][0] = min(dp[i-1][0], dp[i-1][1]) + cnt[i]
    dp[i][1] = min(
        dp[i-1][0] + abs(cnt[i] - k),
        dp[i-1][1] + abs(cnt[i] + cnt[i-1] - k)
    )
```

Let's see if there is an even simpler formula for add:
For current present with available from previous s (where s = prev_c if prev absent else max(0, prev_c-k)):
add = max(0, c - k) + max(0, k - c - s)
This can be interpreted as:
- If c >= k: dispose surplus c-k.
- Else need k-c; use up to s from previous; insert remaining.
Equivalent to:
If c >= k: c-k
Else: max(0, k - c - s)
But note if c >= k, s unused. Could there be benefit to use s to replace current chars and shift current surplus to next? For current present, if c>k, current has surplus. Could we shift some current surplus to next and use previous surplus to fill current target? That would mean current final k composed partly of previous chars, and current own chars shifted to next. This might be beneficial if next needs chars and previous surplus would otherwise be deleted. Example: prev present has surplus s, current c>k, next needs chars. Instead of deleting current surplus and deleting prev surplus, we could shift current surplus to next and use prev surplus at current. Cost? Let's examine.

Our transition assumes if c>=k, current uses its own chars for target and disposes surplus c-k; previous surplus s is disposed (cost already included). Alternative: use x previous surplus at current, and shift x current chars to next. Since current own chars and previous chars are interchangeable for final counts? Operation counts: shifting current char to next costs1; shifting previous char to current costs1 (already paid as disposal). If we use previous surplus at current and shift current surplus to next, we still dispose c-k surplus? Let's model.

Suppose previous present has surplus s available at current at no extra cost (disposal cost paid). Current present target k, current own c. Next present may need chars. If c > k, current has surplus c-k. If we keep k current chars, previous surplus s deleted. If next needs, current surplus can be shifted to next (cost c-k) instead of deleted (cost c-k), same cost. So no difference. If we instead use previous surplus for current and shift some current chars to next, we still have total surplus from previous+current = s + c - k. To satisfy current target k, we need choose k chars from combined s+c; remaining s+c-k must be disposed/shifted. Cost for disposal/shift of remaining is s+c-k? Wait previous surplus disposal cost s already included in dp_prev. Current surplus disposal cost c-k included in add. If we shift some current chars to next, cost same as disposal. Total cost remains dp_prev + (c-k). Using previous surplus doesn't change cost because previous surplus cost s is already paid whether deleted or shifted to current. But if we use previous surplus at current, then current chars shifted to next cost c-k (instead of deleted), same. So no benefit. If next needs, shifting current surplus to next is already allowed in future via availability from current present = max(0,c-k)? Wait if current present and c>k, availability to next = c-k. But if we used previous surplus at current and shifted current chars to next, current availability to next would be c - (k - x) = c-k+x, not just c-k. However, the previous surplus x used at current came from previous; if we instead didn't use it at current, it would be deleted (cost already paid). Shifting it to next would require extra shift from current to next? Actually previous surplus is at current (shifted from prev to current) if we choose to shift it; if not, it's deleted at prev. If we shift previous surplus to current and then to next, cost for that unit: shift prev->current (1) + shift current->next (1) =2, but disposal cost at prev was 1. Total2, equal to delete at prev + insert at next (2). So no need. Thus availability from current present should not include previous surplus passed through. Good.

Let's maybe derive exact DP and see if recurrence is indeed optimal. We can attempt to prove by induction with invariant:
After processing letters 0..i, dp0/dp1 are min costs for a transformation of prefix counts to some final counts for letters 0..i, where:
- dp0: letter i is absent; and no characters are carried from i to i+1? Wait if letter i absent, we might shift its own cnt[i] to i+1. In our state, we don't store carry but we know availability to next = cnt[i]. The cost dp0 includes disposing cnt[i] (either delete or shift to i+1). So it's not "no carry"; it's "can provide cnt[i] to next at no extra cost".
- dp1: letter i is present; can provide max(0,cnt[i]-k) to next at no extra cost.
But if dp0 doesn't include carry from previous, then availability to next is cnt[i]. If dp1 doesn't include pass-through, availability to next is max(0,cnt[i]-k). This invariant is enough for transitions.

Need ensure when computing dp0 for current absent, if previous state dp1 had cost that assumed shifting surplus to current? We choose not to; but dp1 cost is same whether surplus deleted or shifted, so valid. If previous state dp0 had cost that assumed shifting its cnt to current? dp0_prev cost cnt[i-1] same whether deleted or shifted to current. If current absent, we choose delete. Valid.

Now, one subtlety: In dp1 transition from previous absent, we use s = cnt[i-1] as available to current. But dp0_prev cost cnt[i-1] includes disposal of all previous absent chars. If we shift x to current, cost x; delete rest cnt[i-1]-x; total cnt[i-1]. So yes, any amount up to cnt[i-1] available at current at no extra cost beyond dp0_prev. But what if previous absent also had availability from i-2? We disallowed, so dp0_prev only includes own cnt[i-1]. Good.

In dp1 transition from previous present, we use s = max(0,cnt[i-1]-k). dp1_prev cost includes disposal of surplus cnt[i-1]-k if any. If we shift x <= s to current, cost x; delete rest s-x; total s. So available. If cnt[i-1] <= k, s=0. But what if previous present had deficit and received from i-2, and then has no surplus. Good.

Thus ignoring pass-through through present doesn't increase min. But could it be strictly beneficial because the previous present's insertion cost is avoided? Now, let's think about implementation details.

Pseudo:
```
class Solution:
    def makeStringGood(self, s: str) -> int:
        cnt = [0]*26
        for ch in s:
            cnt[ord(ch)-97] += 1
        maxc = max(cnt)
        ans = len(s)  # or large
        for k in range(1, maxc+1):
            dp0 = cnt[0]  # letter 0 absent
            dp1 = abs(cnt[0] - k)  # letter 0 present
            for i in range(1, 26):
                c = cnt[i]
                pc = cnt[i-1]
                # current absent
                ndp0 = min(dp0, dp1) + c
                # current present from prev absent
                add_from_absent = max(0, c - k) + max(0, k - c - pc)
                # current present from prev present
                s = max(0, pc - k)
                add_from_present = max(0, c - k) + max(0, k - c - s)
                ndp1 = min(dp0 + add_from_absent, dp1 + add_from_present)
                dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1
            ans = min(ans, min(dp0, dp1))
        return ans
```
Need ensure dp0/dp1 for i=0 when cnt[0] maybe 0. If letter0 absent cost0, present cost k (insert k). Good.

But wait: For i=0, if letter0 absent, availability to next = cnt[0] = 0. If cnt0=0, no availability. If cnt0>0, dp0=cnt0. Good.

i1 cnt0 prev0:
dp0=min(0,3)+0=0.
dp1=min(
 from dp0=0 + add(c=0, prev_c=0): 0+3=3 =>3,
 from dp1=3 + add(c=0, s=0):0+3=3 =>6
)=3.
...
After i24? Let's process up to z. For all zero letters before z, dp0 stays0, dp1 maybe? At i24 (y cnt0 prev0): dp0=0, dp1=3. At i25 z cnt3 prev0:
dp0=min(0,3)+3=3.
dp1=min(
 from dp0=0 + add(c=3, prev_c=0): c>=k => add=0; total0,
 from dp1=3 + add(c=3, s=0): add0; total3
)=0.
ans0. Good.

Now, what if z has surplus and target k smaller, e.g., "zzzz" cnt z=4. k=2 answer? Delete2 z cost2, or change z? cannot change z to next, so only delete/insert. Good if z present freq2 cost2. DP should handle z surplus by deletion. At i25 c=4,k=2, add max(0,4-2)=2. Good.

Now, let's think about the operation "Change a character in s to its next letter in the alphabet." It says "to its next letter" singular, maybe only one step, but can perform any number of times, so yes multiple.

Now, is there any possibility to change a char to next letter and then delete it? That would be wasteful unless needed for count? No, deletion cost plus change cost > deletion alone. Our model wouldn't do that because shifting then deleting costs2 vs delete1. In DP, we avoid shifting units that are eventually deleted? Let's see: In local cost, if a unit is carried to next and then deleted, total cost2, which is worse than deleting at origin. But because local cost at origin is same whether shift or delete, if it's later deleted, DP might overcount? Wait exact DP with carry allowed shifting then deleting, but min would avoid because cost higher. Our recurrence might inadvertently count a unit as shifted (cost paid) and then not used, but if not used, it would be deleted later adding cost. We don't explicitly track deletion later for carried surplus if not used? Let's examine.

In recurrence, when previous present has surplus s, dp1_prev cost includes disposal cost s (could be deletion or shift). If we transition to current present and use only part x of s, the remaining s-x is not used. Did we account for its deletion? dp1_prev cost s includes deletion of remaining s-x (if not shifted). If we shifted x to current, cost x; remaining s-x deleted cost s-x; total s. So yes, all surplus disposed. If current present doesn't use s, dp1_prev cost s = deletion all. Good.
If previous absent, dp0_prev cost pc includes disposal of all previous chars. If current present uses x, remaining pc-x deleted. Good.
If current absent, dp0 cost c includes disposal of current chars. If next present uses some, remaining deleted. Good.
So no unit is shifted and then deleted with extra cost because the shift cost and deletion cost are part of the same disposal cost at the source? Wait if a unit is shifted from i-1 to i and then not used at i, but maybe used at i+1. If it is shifted again to i+1, cost for that unit so far: shift i-1->i (1) + shift i->i+1 (1) =2. In our recurrence, if previous absent at i-1, dp0_prev cost pc includes shift i-1->i for x units. Then current absent? If current absent, we don't carry previous surplus through current, so those x units would not be shifted to i+1; they would be deleted at current? But if current absent, we said previous surplus should not be shifted into current. So no pass-through. If current present and uses x, no further shift. If current present and has surplus, we don't use previous surplus to create additional surplus. So a unit from previous is never shifted more than one step in our restricted solution. Is that always optimal? What about a unit from i-1 to i+1 (distance2) equal to delete+insert; ignoring okay. What about distance1 from i-1 to i, we allow. What about a unit from i-2 to i (distance2) through i-1 present/absent, equal to delete+insert; ignoring okay. Thus we never need shift any unit more than one step? Wait what about source at i-2 absent, target i present, distance2. We ignore, but cost equal to delete+insert. So min unaffected. What about source at i-2 present surplus, target i present, distance2. Equal. What about source at i-3, distance3, worse. Therefore optimal can be achieved with shifts only between adjacent letters where the source letter is either absent and target is immediate next present, or source present surplus to immediate next present. No shift over two edges. This is a key insight. Our recurrence embodies that. Good.

But is it true that shifting one step is always beneficial compared to delete+insert? Yes cost1 vs2. So we should allow adjacent shifts. Shifting two steps cost2 equal to delete+insert, so optional. Shifting >2 worse. Thus restricting to adjacent shifts doesn't increase cost. Nice.

Let's prove: Any operation sequence can be transformed to one where each original/inserted character is shifted at most one position? Wait if a char is shifted two positions, cost2. Replacing by delete at original and insert at destination cost2, same. If shifted more, replacement cheaper. So there exists optimal with no char shifted more than one position? But if a char shifted two positions and along the way maybe used? It wasn't used at intermediate because if it stopped, distance <=1? Actually if shifted from i to i+1 and then i+1 to i+2, it passes through i+1. If i+1 final count includes it, then it stopped at i+1 (distance1). If not, it's pass-through. Replace pass-through by delete at i and insert at i+2 cost2. So yes, no pass-through beyond one edge. Thus each unit either stays at original letter, moves exactly one step to next letter, or is deleted/inserted. This simplifies to adjacent transfer only. Great.

Now, with adjacent transfer only, we can model each letter's own count plus possible transfer from previous. Our recurrence is exact.

Let's verify with a case where shifting two steps equal to delete+insert but maybe combined with other operations could be strictly better due to avoiding insertion at intermediate? If a unit passes through an intermediate letter that is present, it could contribute to intermediate's count while passing? No, if it contributes, it stops there. If it passes, it doesn't. So no.

Now, let's consider if an inserted character can be inserted at one letter and then shifted to next? That would cost insert1 + shift1 =2, equivalent to inserting directly at next cost1. So never beneficial. Our model doesn't insert then shift. Good.

Similarly, a deleted character could be shifted before deletion, cost2 vs delete1, never beneficial. So no shift-then-delete. Our recurrence doesn't shift units that are ultimately deleted beyond the one step? Wait if previous surplus shifted to current and current doesn't use it, it would be deleted at current, cost shift1 + delete1 =2, worse than delete at previous. Our recurrence when current doesn't use previous surplus doesn't add deletion at current; it just leaves it deleted at previous. Good.

Now, let's think about the DP transition from previous absent to current present when c > k. We add c-k and ignore previous availability. Set up: letter i-1 previous has availability s (from absent or present surplus) that can move to i at cost already counted. Letter i current has c > k. Letter i+1 next present needs k. Now, let's maybe derive an exact min-cost flow and see if recurrence matches for all cases by considering "no shift more than one" lemma. Let's prove lemma more rigorously:

Given any sequence of operations, consider each original character. It may be deleted at some point, or inserted? Original chars can be changed multiple times (moved right) and then maybe deleted. If a character is changed at least twice before being deleted or ending, suppose it moves from i to j (j>i) and ends at j or deleted. If it ends at j and j-i >=2, replace by deleting it at i and inserting a new character at j. Cost of changes j-i; replacement cost delete1+insert1=2. If j-i=2, equal; if >2, cheaper. If it is deleted after moving, moving before deletion costs extra, so delete at original cheaper. Thus there exists optimal where each original character is changed at most once, and if changed, it is not deleted later. Similarly, inserted characters are never changed. Therefore operations can be seen as: each original char either stays, moves one step right, or is deleted; insertions create chars at final positions. Wait if a char moves one step right and then later another char? It cannot move more than one. So yes, each original char moves at most one step. But what about a char that moves from i to i+1 and then stays; that's one step. A char that moves from i to i+2 would be two steps, replaced. Thus optimal with at most one shift per char. This is stronger than "no pass-through"; it means no char moves more than one position. Is that always true? Let's test example [10,0,0], k=5, choose c present, a,b absent. We said cost15 via delete+insert or shift two steps equal. There is optimal with no char moving more than one: delete all10 a cost10, insert5 c cost5 total15. Yes. Example [10,0,0], k=5, choose b present: shift5 a->b (one step), delete5 a cost5 total10. Good. Example [10,0,0,0], k=5, choose d present: delete10+insert5=15; no shift >1. Good. So lemma holds.

If each original char moves at most one step, then transfers only between adjacent letters. Then we can model each letter i has original cnt[i] plus inflow from i-1 (chars moved from i-1 to i) and outflow to i+1 (chars moved from i to i+1). But if a char moves from i-1 to i, it cannot then move to i+1. Thus outflow from i to i+1 must come from original chars at i, not from inflow. Wait in our recurrence, when previous absent shifts chars to current, those chars stay at current; they cannot be shifted further. When current present has surplus, outflow to next comes from current's original chars, not from inflow. This matches our availability from current present = max(0,cnt[i]-k) (original surplus), not including inflow. But what if current original count c < k and it receives inflow to meet target, then no outflow. If c > k, it doesn't need inflow, and outflow c-k. If c = k, no inflow/outflow. This is exactly the recurrence! Great. This lemma is key.

Let's verify with a scenario where a char moves from i to i+1 and then another char from i+1 to i+2; that's two different chars, allowed. Our recurrence allows current surplus (original at current) to shift to next, while previous chars may shift into current. So each char at most one step. Good.

Now, with at most one shift per char, the problem becomes: For each letter i, original cnt[i] can be allocated to:
- stay at i (contribute to final f_i)
- shift to i+1 (contribute to final f_{i+1})
- delete
Additionally, insertions at i can contribute to final f_i.
No char can shift from i-1 to i and then to i+1. Thus inflow to i from i-1 can only stay at i, not pass through. This is exactly our DP.

Let's re-evaluate recurrence in this light:
At letter i, we have:
- cnt[i] original chars.
- inflow x from i-1 (chars shifted from i-1 to i) if previous letter absent/present. Inflow can only stay at i or be deleted? Wait if a char shifts from i-1 to i, it cannot shift further. If not used at i, it would be deleted at i, but shifting then deleting is wasteful compared to deleting at i-1. So in optimal, inflow x is exactly the amount needed at i (or maybe less? If previous absent, we might shift x to i and if not used, delete at i; but we can choose not to shift and delete at previous, same cost. So we can assume inflow is used at i). Thus inflow contributes to final f_i.
- Outflow y from i to i+1 comes from cnt[i] original chars not staying at i. They move one step and will be used at i+1 (not deleted).
- Deletions of cnt[i] not used.
- Insertions at i if needed.

For letter i, final f_i is either 0 or k.
If f_i = 0: no inflow should be used; previous chars that might have shifted to i are not needed, so choose inflow0. All cnt[i] must be either deleted or shifted to i+1. Since shifting to i+1 costs same as deletion, we can decide outflow y later; cost for cnt[i] is cnt[i]. This matches dp0 add c.
If f_i = k: Need k chars at i. Available sources: inflow from previous (cost already paid by previous disposal), original cnt[i] staying, insertions. Also, some original cnt[i] may be shifted to i+1 instead of staying. Let y be outflow to i+1 from cnt[i]. Then number of original cnt[i] staying = cnt[i] - y (if cnt[i]>=y). Need total k = inflow + (cnt[i]-y) + insertions. Cost for current letter: y (shift to next) + insertions + deletions? Deletions of cnt[i] not staying/shifting? Actually cnt[i] allocated: y shifted, z stay, d delete, y+z+d=cnt[i]. Cost y + d + insertions. Inflow cost already paid. We want minimize. Since y and d both cost1 per char, total cost for cnt[i] not staying is cnt[i]-z. Final k = inflow + z + insertions. Let A = cnt[i] + inflow. If we choose z = min(cnt[i], k - inflow) maybe. But if cnt[i] > k - inflow, we have surplus cnt[i] - (k-inflow) that can be shifted or deleted, cost same. If cnt[i] < k - inflow, need insertions. So current cost = max(0, cnt[i] - (k - inflow))? Wait if cnt[i] > needed, surplus cost cnt[i] - needed. If cnt[i] < needed, insertion cost needed - cnt[i]. This is abs(cnt[i] - (k - inflow))? Let's compute: needed from cnt[i] after inflow = k - inflow (assuming inflow <= k). If inflow can exceed k? We wouldn't take more inflow than needed because inflow chars would be deleted at i (waste) or shift? They can't shift further. So inflow <= k. Then current cost = |cnt[i] - (k - inflow)|? If cnt[i] > k-inflow, surplus cost cnt[i] - (k-inflow). If cnt[i] < k-inflow, insertion cost (k-inflow)-cnt[i]. Yes. But our recurrence add = max(0,cnt[i]-k) + max(0,k-cnt[i]-s), where s=inflow. Is that equal to |cnt[i] - (k - s)|? Let's check:
If cnt[i] >= k: add = cnt[i]-k. |cnt[i] - (k-s)| = cnt[i]-k+s if s>0, not equal. Wait because if inflow s>0 and cnt[i]>=k, total available cnt[i]+s > k; surplus cnt[i]+s-k. But inflow chars cannot be shifted further, so if we take inflow s, we would have surplus s that must be deleted at i, costing extra. Thus we should take inflow 0 when cnt[i]>=k. Our recurrence add = cnt[i]-k, not cnt[i]+s-k. It assumes inflow0. Good. So current cost formula depends on choosing inflow optimally: if cnt[i] >= k, inflow0, cost cnt[i]-k. If cnt[i] < k, need k-cnt[i], take up to s inflow, insert rest, cost max(0,k-cnt[i]-s). This is our add. Great.

This also shows why if cnt[i]>=k, previous inflow not used, and outflow to next is cnt[i]-k (original surplus). If cnt[i]<k, no outflow. Good.

Now, what about if cnt[i]>=k but we could use inflow and shift some original cnt[i] to next, each char moves at most one step. Inflow from previous stays at i, original cnt[i] shifted to next. This would allow using previous surplus and current surplus simultaneously. But final f_i=k. Suppose inflow s>0, outflow y>0. Then total available at i = cnt[i]+s. Keep k, surplus cnt[i]+s-k. This surplus can be either deleted or shifted? Outflow can only be original cnt[i], not inflow. If we shift y original to next, and use inflow s to help keep k, the total kept from original is k-s. We need cnt[i] - y = k - s (assuming no insertion/deletion of kept). So y = cnt[i] - k + s. Cost for current original cnt[i]: y shifted + deletions of remaining? cnt[i] allocated: y shift, k-s stay, rest delete. Total cost for cnt[i] = y + (cnt[i] - y - (k-s)) = cnt[i] - (k-s) = cnt[i]-k+s. Plus inflow cost already paid. Compare to not using inflow: cost cnt[i]-k, inflow deleted at previous (cost s already paid). Total previous cost includes s either way. If use inflow and shift current surplus, total cost = previous disposal s + current cost (cnt[i]-k+s) = previous disposal s + cnt[i]-k+s = previous disposal + cnt[i]-k + s. If not use inflow, total = previous disposal s + cnt[i]-k. So using inflow is worse by s (because inflow chars are used at i but they displace current chars that then shift to next; the displaced current chars cost shift, and inflow chars cost shift from previous; total two shifts for one final position? Actually previous disposal s paid regardless, but if inflow used, previous chars moved to i; if not, they deleted. The cost s is paid either way. Using inflow adds no extra beyond s? Wait previous disposal s is paid either way. Current cost using inflow = cnt[i]-k+s. Not using = cnt[i]-k. So using inflow adds s to current cost, but previous disposal s was already paid in both. Total using = prev_s + current = prev_s + cnt[i]-k+s; not using = prev_s + cnt[i]-k. So using is worse by s. Thus never use inflow when cnt[i]>=k. Good. This matches recurrence and availability.

What if cnt[i]<k, but we could still shift some original cnt[i] to next and use inflow to fill, while also inserting? That would be silly because original shifted to next costs1, inflow used at i costs already, and insertion maybe; better to keep original at i and not shift. So no outflow when cnt[i]<k. Good.

Thus recurrence is exact under at-most-one-shift lemma.

Now, let's think about if a char can move from i to i+1 and then be part of final string at i+1, while another char at i+1 moves to i+2. This is allowed; our DP handles via outflow from current and inflow to current separately. For letter i+1, if cnt[i+1] < k, it may receive inflow from i and have no outflow. If cnt[i+1] >= k, it won't receive inflow and may have outflow. If cnt[i+1] = k, no inflow/outflow. This creates a kind of alternating flow: surplus from a letter with high count can move right to a letter with low count; if next next also low, it may need insertion because the low letter doesn't pass through. But could a chain of surpluses move right through multiple low letters? Each char moves at most one step, so a surplus from i can only satisfy i+1, not i+2. If i+1 is low and uses it, i+2 must be satisfied by its own surplus or insertion. If i+1 is high and has surplus, it can satisfy i+2. So flow is only between adjacent letters. This is consistent.

Let's test a chain: counts [10,0,10,0], k=5, choose all present. Recurrence:

Total10. Exact: a10 -> keep5, shift5 to b; c10 -> keep5, shift5 to d; cost10. Good. If we tried to shift a surplus to c through b (distance2), cost extra; not needed.

Now, let's think about if there is any benefit to not keeping a letter present even if its count equals k, to use its chars to help next? If count equals k and we choose absent, all k chars can be shifted to next (cost k) or deleted (cost k), and next can use them. But then final absent, next present. Compare to keeping it present (cost0) and next maybe insert k (cost k). Total k either way. Example counts a5,b0,k=5. Choose a present only cost0; choose a absent,b present cost5 (shift5 a->b). Suppose a5,b5,k=5, choose a absent,b present: delete/shift a5 cost5, b present cost0 total5; choose a,b present cost0. So no. Our DP will choose min.

Now, let's consider if there is a possibility to change a char from a to b, and another from b to c, effectively moving surplus from a to c via b's original char? No, each char independent. Our model with at most one shift per char means a surplus from a can only satisfy b, not c. But if b has its own surplus, it can satisfy c. This is like a chain of adjacent transfers. If a surplus needs to go to c and b has no surplus, delete+insert is equal/better. Good.

Now, let's maybe derive the DP from the "at most one shift" lemma in a way that ensures no double-counting of costs.

Detailed cost accounting for a fixed k:
We maintain after processing letter i-1:
- `dp0`: min cost for letters < i, with letter i-1 absent. This cost includes:
  * For each processed letter j < i-1, their final statuses.
  * For letter i-1 absent, all its cnt[i-1] chars are disposed. The disposal cost cnt[i-1] is included. If we later want to shift some of them to i, we can do so at no extra cost because the disposal cost is the same as shifting. Thus they are "available" to i.
  * No chars from earlier letters are available to i (because if an earlier letter shifted to i-1 and i-1 absent, that would be a two-step shift for those chars, not needed; or if i-1 absent, it doesn't pass through).
- `dp1`: min cost with letter i-1 present. This cost includes:
  * For letter i-1, if cnt[i-1] > k, surplus cnt[i-1]-k is disposed. If we later want to shift some surplus to i, no extra cost. Thus availability = max(0,cnt[i-1]-k).
  * If cnt[i-1] <= k, no surplus available.
  * If letter i-1 received chars from i-2, those chars are used to meet its target and not available to i; no pass-through.

When processing letter i:
- If i absent: We must dispose cnt[i] chars. They can be shifted to i+1 later, but no chars from i-1 are used. Cost +cnt[i]. New state absent.
- If i present:
  * Let `avail` be chars available from i-1:
    - if i-1 absent: avail = cnt[i-1]
    - if i-1 present: avail = max(0, cnt[i-1]-k)
  * If cnt[i] >= k: We don't use avail (using it would only create extra surplus at i, since those chars cannot pass through). We keep k of cnt[i], dispose cnt[i]-k (can shift to i+1 later). Cost +cnt[i]-k.
  * If cnt[i] < k: We need k-cnt[i] additional chars. Use up to avail from previous, insert the rest. Cost +max(0, k-cnt[i]-avail).
This is exactly our add formula:
`add = max(0, cnt[i]-k) + max(0, k-cnt[i]-avail)`.
Note if cnt[i]>=k, second term 0. If cnt[i]<k, first term0. Good.

This accounting is clear.

Now, let's think about if `dp0` for letter i absent should include cost cnt[i] even if cnt[i] chars are shifted to i+1 later. Yes, disposal cost cnt[i] is same as shifting all or deleting. So if later i+1 present uses some, no extra cost. This is why in transition from previous absent to current present, avail=cnt[i-1] and no extra cost beyond dp0_prev. Good.

Now, let's think about if `dp1` for letter i present with cnt[i]>=k includes cost cnt[i]-k for surplus disposal. If later i+1 present uses surplus, no extra cost. If not, deleted. Good.

Now, let's think about if `dp1` for letter i present with cnt[i]<k includes insertion cost for deficit. If later i+1 present, no surplus. Good.

Now, let's think about if a letter i present with cnt[i]>=k and previous avail >0, we choose not to use avail. But dp1_prev cost included disposal of avail (if previous present surplus) or previous absent cnt. If previous present surplus not used, it is deleted at previous. If previous absent cnt not used, deleted at previous. Cost already included. Good.

Now, let's think about if a letter i present with cnt[i]<k and avail > deficit, we use only deficit, remaining avail deleted at previous. Cost already included. Good.

Now, let's think about if a letter i present with cnt[i]<k and avail = 0, insert deficit. Good.

Now, let's think about if a letter i absent and previous avail >0, we delete previous avail at previous. Cost already included. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "insert a character in s" cost1, but if we insert at i and later change to i+1, cost2 vs insert at i+1 cost1. We never do. Good.

Now, let's think about if we need to consider changing a char from i to i+1 and then that char is part of final string at i+1, while another char originally at i+1 is shifted to i+2. This is allowed and our DP handles because for letter i+1, if cnt[i+1] >= k, it doesn't use inflow and shifts its own surplus; if cnt[i+1] < k, it uses inflow and doesn't shift. But what if cnt[i+1] >= k and there is inflow from i (because previous letter present surplus), we choose not to use inflow. But what if cnt[i+1] >= k and we still want to shift some of its own chars to i+2, and also use inflow to keep k? We argued using inflow when cnt>=k is worse by inflow amount. Let's re-evaluate with at-most-one-shift: Inflow chars from i to i+1 cannot pass to i+2. If cnt[i+1]>=k and we use inflow x at i+1, then we must shift x of cnt[i+1] to i+2 to keep final k. Cost: inflow x already paid (as disposal at i), shift x from i+1 to i+2 cost x. If instead delete inflow at i (cost x already paid) and keep cnt[i+1] at i+1, no shift to i+2. The latter saves x shift cost. So using inflow when cnt>=k is worse. Thus optimal no inflow. Good.

What if cnt[i+1]>=k but i+2 needs chars and would otherwise insert; using inflow at i+1 and shifting i+1 surplus to i+2 could satisfy i+2 without insertion, but costs x shift from i+1 to i+2. Alternative delete inflow at i (cost x) and insert at i+2 (cost x) =2x; using inflow+shift costs inflow x + shift x =2x. Equal. So no strict benefit. Our DP may miss equal-cost solution but min unaffected. If distance from i to i+2 is2, equal. Good.

Now, let's think about if using inflow when cnt>=k could be strictly better if the alternative insert at i+2 cost x but delete inflow at i cost x, total2x; using inflow+shift also2x. Equal. So okay.

Now, let's consider if there is any case where using inflow when cnt>=k and shifting current surplus to next is strictly better because current surplus would otherwise be deleted (cost x) and inflow deleted (cost x), total2x; using inflow+shift also2x. Equal. So no.

Thus recurrence exact.

Now, let's think about if `dp0` for letter i absent should maybe include availability from previous if previous absent? We disallow pass-through through absent, but if previous absent and current absent, previous chars could shift to current and then current chars shift to next? That would be two shifts for previous chars, equal to delete+insert. No strict. So no.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with letters before first nonzero and dp0/dp1 initialization. Suppose cnt[0]=0. dp0=0, dp1=k. If k large, dp1 present a with insert k. But if later b has surplus, could a be present and receive? No backward, so a present would require insert k, likely not optimal. DP can choose dp0=0. Good.

Now, let's think about if target k loop from 1 to max(cnt) inclusive. If max(cnt) = 0 impossible. Good.

 from dp1=0 + add(c=0, s=max(0,2-2)=0): insert2 =>2
)=2.
i2 c2 prev0:
dp0=min(0,2)+2=2.
dp1=min(
 from dp0=0 + add(c=2, prev_c=0): add0; total0,
 from dp1=2 + add(c=2, s=0): add0; total2
)=0.
ans0. Good.

Now, let's think about if all nonzero counts equal but some letter with count0 present could be inserted to make more letters, not optimal. DP min0.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "s contains only lowercase English letters" and alphabet size 26. Good.

Now, let's consider if we can optimize by breaking early? Not needed.

Now, let's think about if there is a possibility that target k is not an integer? No.

Now, let's think about if operation "Delete a character from s" can delete any character, including inserted? Yes, but not beneficial.

Now, let's think about if operation "Insert a character in s" can insert any lowercase letter, and "Change a character in s to its next letter" can change inserted char, but not beneficial.

Now, let's maybe think about a formal proof of at-most-one-shift:
- Consider an optimal sequence. For each original character, trace its final position or deletion. If it is changed at least twice, it moves from i to j with j-i>=2. If it is deleted after changes, remove all changes and delete it at original: cost decreases. If it ends at j, replace the changes by deleting it at i and inserting a new character at j. Cost changes from (j-i) to 2. If j-i=2, equal; if >2, lower. Repeating yields optimal with no original char changed more than once. Inserted chars need not be changed because inserting directly at final position is cheaper. Thus each original char moves at most one step. Good.

Now, with at-most-one-shift, a char that moves from i to i+1 cannot move further. Therefore inflow to i from i-1 cannot be passed to i+1. This is the key.

Now, let's think about if a char can be changed from i to i+1 and then the same char later changed from i+1 to i+2 in an optimal sequence if j-i=2 and cost equal; but there exists another optimal without it. So our DP finds min, not necessarily all.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "insert a character" creating a char that is then changed one step; cost2 vs insert at target cost1, so no.

Now, let's think about if "delete a character" after changing one step cost2 vs delete at original cost1, so no.

Thus the restricted operation set is equivalent in cost.

Now, let's consider if there is any need to account for changing a char from i to i+1 and then that char is deleted at i+1 because i+1 absent? We avoid by deleting at i. Good.

Now, let's think about if our DP's `dp0` for absent letter includes cost cnt[i] but if cnt[i] chars are shifted to i+1 and then i+1 absent, they'd be deleted at i+1 cost extra. But we can choose not to shift them; cost same at i. So no issue.

Now, let's think about if `dp1` for present letter with cnt[i]>k includes cost cnt[i]-k; if those surplus chars are shifted to i+1 and i+1 absent, they'd be deleted at i+1 extra. But we can choose to delete at i; cost same. So no issue.

Now, let's think about if there is any scenario where shifting surplus from i to i+1 and then i+1 absent but i+2 present could be equal to delete+insert; we ignore, min unaffected. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the order of operations: If we change a to b and delete some a, operations commute. Counts model enough.

Now, let's think about if the final string t can have characters that were originally inserted and then changed? No need.

Now, let's think about if there is any possibility to change a char from i to i+1 and then insert a char at i to replace it, effectively moving count right and inserting at source. That's equivalent to shifting one char right and inserting one at source; our model allows because original char at i shifted to i+1 and insertion at i. Good.

Now, let's think about if a letter i present with cnt[i]<k and previous avail insufficient, we insert at i. Could we instead shift some original char from i to i+1 and insert more at i? No, would increase cost and not help final i. Good.

Now, let's think about if a letter i present with cnt[i]>=k and previous avail exists, we delete previous avail and delete current surplus. Could we instead shift current surplus to i+1 and use previous avail at i, cost same as deleting current surplus? Actually using previous avail at i and shifting current surplus to i+1 costs previous disposal (already) + current shift. Deleting current surplus costs current deletion. Same. But if i+1 needs chars, shifting current surplus to i+1 is beneficial compared to deleting current surplus and inserting at i+1. Our DP allows current surplus to be available to i+1 via `dp1` state (availability = cnt[i]-k). It doesn't matter whether the surplus chars are "current" or "previous" because counts at i+1 just need chars; if we used previous at i and shifted current to i+1, the total number of chars available to i+1 is still cnt[i]-k (not plus previous). Wait if previous avail x is used at i, then current chars shifted to i+1 = x? Let's set cnt[i]=10,k=5, prev avail=5. Current surplus =5. If we use prev avail 5 at i, we can shift 5 current to i+1. Availability to i+1 =5. If we don't use prev, we delete prev5 and shift current5 to i+1. Availability to i+1 =5. Same. So state availability = cnt[i]-k. Good.

If cnt[i]<k and prev avail > deficit, could we use all prev avail at i and shift some current? Current cnt[i] all needed? If cnt[i]<k, even with all prev avail, total maybe >k. Example cnt[i]=3,k=5, prev avail=5. Need2 from prev, remaining3 prev deleted. Could we use all5 prev at i and shift3 current to i+1? Then final i would be5 (all prev), current shifted3 to i+1. This would provide 3 chars to i+1, whereas our DP says current cnt<k so no availability to next. Is this allowed with at-most-one-shift? Prev chars move from i-1 to i (one step), current chars move from i to i+1 (one step). Yes! This is a potential alternative: use previous surplus to fill current, and shift current's own chars to next. We earlier considered this and found cost? Let's analyze carefully. This could be beneficial if next needs chars, and previous surplus would otherwise be deleted. It doesn't involve any char moving more than one step. Did our recurrence miss this? Very important!

Let's set up: Letter i has cnt[i] = c < k. Previous availability s > k - c (more than enough to fill deficit). If we use only deficit from previous, remaining previous surplus s - (k-c) is deleted at previous (cost already paid). Current has no outflow. If instead we use all s previous at i, and shift some current chars to i+1, final i = k. Since total available at i = c + s. Need keep k, surplus c+s-k can be shifted to i+1 (current chars) or deleted. But wait current chars shifted to i+1 are part of surplus; previous chars used at i. This is allowed. Cost: previous disposal s already paid. Current original c: if we shift y = c+s-k chars to i+1, and keep k-s chars of current? Let's compute. We need final i = k. Use s previous at i. Then need k-s current chars to stay at i. Current c must satisfy c = (k-s) + y + d, where y shifted to next, d deleted. Since c+s > k, surplus c+s-k. We can shift y = c+s-k (all surplus) to next, d=0, and keep k-s current. Cost for current: y shift = c+s-k. If instead use only deficit from previous: use k-c previous, delete remaining previous s-(k-c), keep all c current, no shift. Cost: previous disposal s + current cost0 = s. Wait previous disposal s is paid in both. Current cost in alternative = c+s-k. Total alternative = s + (c+s-k) = c+2s-k. Original = s. Alternative is more expensive by c+s-k >0. But it provides y = c+s-k chars to next, which could save insertions at next. If next needs y chars, saving insertion cost y. Net difference: alternative total - original total = (c+2s-k) - s = c+s-k = y. Saving insertion y. Net equal! If next needs less than y, alternative worse. If next needs exactly y, equal. If next needs more, alternative still only provides y, additional insert. So using all previous surplus and shifting current surplus to next is never strictly better than deleting previous surplus and inserting at next? Let's compare:
Original: delete surplus previous (s-(k-c)) at previous, keep current, next inserts y (if needed) cost y.
Alternative: shift all previous to current, shift y current to next. Cost difference: previous disposal same s; current shift y vs next insert y. Both cost y. Equal. So no strict benefit. Thus ignoring is okay. But wait original also deletes surplus previous s-(k-c) cost included in s. Alternative doesn't delete previous surplus (uses it), but shifts current y. The costs balance. So min unaffected. Good.

What if next needs chars and would otherwise be satisfied by its own surplus? Then alternative unnecessary. So no strict.

Thus our recurrence's rule "if c<k, no outflow" is valid up to equal-cost alternatives. It may miss equal-cost solutions but min same. Good.

But let's test a concrete case where this equal alternative could affect later DP state availability? Suppose c<k, previous surplus large, next present needs chars. Our DP says current has no availability to next, so next may insert. Alternative could provide y chars to next, reducing insertions, but costs y extra at current. Net equal. So DP cost remains min. Let's verify with example.

Counts: i0 absent cnt10, i1 present cnt3, i2 present cnt0, k=5. Our recurrence:
i0 absent dp0=10.
i1 present from prev absent: c=3<5, avail=10, need2, add0, dp1=10. (a absent, b present: shift2 a->b, delete8 a cost10)
i2 present from prev present s=max(0,3-5)=0, c=0, add insert5, dp=15.
Alternative: shift10 a->b? Wait i0 absent cnt10, i1 cnt3. To provide chars to i2, use all10 a at b? But a chars shift to b (one step), b own3. Need b final5, so keep5, surplus8? Actually b available 13, keep5, shift8 to c. But b own3 + a10 =13. Need b5, so surplus8 can shift to c. c needs5, so shift5 to c, delete3. Cost: shift10 a->b cost10, shift5 b->c cost5, delete3 b? Wait b own3 + a10 =13, keep5, shift5 to c, delete3. Total cost10+5+3=18. Our DP15. Alternative not better. What about use only5 a to b? b3+5=8, keep5, shift3 to c, delete2 a? c needs5, insert2. Cost shift5 a->b (5), shift3 b->c (3), delete5 a? Let's compute i0 a10: shift5 to b, delete5 cost10. b3+5=8, keep5, shift3 to c cost3, c needs2 insert2. Total15. Same as DP. So equal.

Our DP chose shift2 a to b, delete8 a, insert5 c cost10+5=15. Alternative shift5 a to b, shift3 b to c, insert2 c cost10+3+2=15. Equal. Good.

Thus recurrence min unaffected.

Now, let's think about if there is a case where using previous surplus to fill current and shifting current surplus to next could be strictly better because it avoids deletion of current surplus? But c<k, current has no surplus relative to k, but if we fill with previous, current chars become surplus. The cost of shifting them equals the cost of inserting at next; the previous surplus used at current would otherwise be deleted. The trade-off is one-for-one. Equal.

Now, let's consider if c>=k and previous surplus exists, using previous at current and shifting current surplus to next equal as discussed. So no strict.

Therefore our DP's state availability from current present = max(0,c-k) is sufficient for min cost, even though there may be alternative ways to provide additional chars to next at equal cost. Since we only need min, ignoring equal alternatives is fine. But wait, could an equal alternative at current enable a strictly better outcome later by changing the "state" in a way that reduces future cost more than the equal cost? If it provides y chars to next at cost y, and next would otherwise insert y at cost y, future cost same. If next doesn't need y, alternative worse. So no future strict benefit. If next needs more than y, alternative provides y and future inserts rest; total same as DP. Thus no.

Now, let's think about if there is a case where using previous surplus to fill current and shifting current surplus to next could reduce deletions at next? Deletions at next are independent of inflow; if next has surplus, it deletes. Inflow to next would add to surplus, not reduce deletions. So no.

Thus recurrence is solid.

Now, let's think about if we should maybe include a more general DP over availability to be safe? But recurrence is simpler and correct. Let's see if exact DP with carry would produce same min. For the example above, exact DP maybe has multiple optimal; recurrence finds one.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with `dp0` for absent letter and availability to next = cnt[i] even if cnt[i] chars are shifted to next and next present, but if next present has c_next >= k, it won't use them; they'd be deleted at next? We choose not to shift, delete at current. Cost same. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possibility to shift chars from an absent letter to next present and then next present shifts its own surplus to next next. This is allowed and our DP handles: absent letter i availability to i+1; if i+1 present with c_{i+1} >= k, it won't use inflow and will shift its own surplus to i+2. The inflow from i is deleted at i (or at i+1? We choose delete at i). Cost same. If c_{i+1} < k, it uses inflow and no outflow. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possibility to shift chars from an absent letter i to i+1 present, and i+1 present also uses its own chars to shift to i+2, while using inflow to fill its target. Now, let's think about if there is a possibility to shift chars from a present letter i with surplus to i+1 present, and i+1 present uses inflow and shifts its own surplus to i+2. This is also equal alternative. DP ignores, min same. Example i cnt10, i+1 cnt3, i+2 cnt0,k5. DP:

Now, let's think about if there is any case where DP overestimates because it deletes previous surplus and inserts at next, while alternative shifts previous surplus through current and shifts current surplus to next, same cost. So no overestimate.

Now, let's consider if there is any case where DP underestimates by using previous surplus at current and not accounting for the fact that previous surplus chars are shifted from previous to current, but if previous was present with surplus, dp1_prev cost included disposal of surplus as if deleted. If we use them at current, we need to actually shift them, cost same as deletion. So no undercount. If previous was absent, dp0_prev cost included disposal of all previous chars; shifting some to current cost same. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `dp0_prev` when previous absent and cnt[i-1] large, and current present uses some, but dp0_prev might have been computed as min(dp0_before, dp1_before)+cnt[i-1]. Now, let's maybe write final code:

```
class Solution:
    def makeStringGood(self, s: str) -> int:
        cnt = [0] * 26
        for ch in s:
            cnt[ord(ch) - 97] += 1
        
        max_cnt = max(cnt)
        ans = len(s)
        
        for k in range(1, max_cnt + 1):
            dp0 = cnt[0]          # letter 0 absent
            dp1 = abs(cnt[0] - k) # letter 0 present
            
            for i in range(1, 26):
                c = cnt[i]
                pc = cnt[i-1]
                
                # letter i absent
                ndp0 = min(dp0, dp1) + c
                
                # letter i present, previous absent
                add0 = max(0, c - k) + max(0, k - c - pc)
                
                # letter i present, previous present
                avail = max(0, pc - k)
                add1 = max(0, c - k) + max(0, k - c - avail)
                
                ndp1 = min(dp0 + add0, dp1 + add1)
                
                dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1
            
            ans = min(ans, min(dp0, dp1))
        
        return ans
```

Need check if `ans = len(s)` is okay if max_cnt loop maybe not run? max_cnt>=1. Good.

"wddw": cnt d2,w2, max2. k=1? maybe cost? d2,w2 target1: delete1 each cost2. k=2 ans0. Good.
"aaabc": max3. k=1 ans2, k=2 ans2, k=3 ans2. Good.

Let's maybe try to prove recurrence by induction more formally to catch any hidden assumption.

Define an optimal solution restricted to no char moves more than one step. For each letter i, let:
- `in_i` = number of chars moved from i-1 to i (only from original at i-1). These chars can only be used at i.
- `out_i` = number of chars moved from i to i+1 (only from original at i). These chars can only be used at i+1.
- `stay_i` = original chars at i that stay at i.
- `del_i` = original chars at i deleted.
- `ins_i` = chars inserted at i.
Then for each i:
cnt[i] = stay_i + out_i + del_i.
Final f_i = in_i + stay_i + ins_i.
Cost = sum(out_i + del_i + ins_i). Note `in_i` cost is counted as `out_{i-1}` or `del_{i-1}`? Actually if a char moves from i-1 to i, it is `out_{i-1}`, cost counted at i-1. If a char from i-1 is deleted, `del_{i-1}`. So `in_i` is part of `out_{i-1}` and no separate cost.
We need f_i ∈ {0,k}.

Now, for a given i, if f_i=0, then in_i + stay_i + ins_i =0, so in_i=0, stay_i=0, ins_i=0. Thus cnt[i] = out_i + del_i. Cost for i = out_i+del_i = cnt[i]. This is independent of out_i. So if absent, cost cnt[i] and can choose out_i any 0..cnt[i] to help i+1. This matches dp0.

If f_i=k, then in_i + stay_i + ins_i = k. We can choose out_i. Since out_i and del_i both cost1, for fixed stay_i, cost out_i+del_i = cnt[i]-stay_i. Total cost for i = cnt[i]-stay_i + ins_i. We want minimize subject to stay_i + ins_i = k - in_i, with 0<=stay_i<=cnt[i], ins_i>=0, out_i=cnt[i]-stay_i-del_i>=0 (del_i>=0). Actually out_i can be any 0..cnt[i]-stay_i; del_i rest. Cost out_i+del_i = cnt[i]-stay_i regardless of out_i. So cost = cnt[i]-stay_i + ins_i. Since stay_i + ins_i = k - in_i, cost = cnt[i] - stay_i + (k - in_i - stay_i) = cnt[i] + k - in_i - 2*stay_i. To minimize, maximize stay_i subject to stay_i <= cnt[i] and stay_i <= k - in_i (because ins_i>=0). Thus stay_i = min(cnt[i], k - in_i), assuming in_i <= k. If in_i > k, then k - in_i negative impossible; so in_i <= k. Then cost = cnt[i] - min(cnt[i], k-in_i) + max(0, k-in_i - cnt[i])? Let's compute:
If cnt[i] >= k-in_i: stay=k-in_i, cost = cnt[i] - (k-in_i) = cnt[i] - k + in_i.
If cnt[i] < k-in_i: stay=cnt[i], cost = k-in_i - cnt[i].
This is |cnt[i] - (k-in_i)|? If cnt>=k-in, cost cnt-k+in = cnt-(k-in). If cnt<, cost k-in-cnt. Yes cost = abs(cnt[i] - (k - in_i)). But wait if in_i >0 and cnt[i] >= k-in_i, cost includes +in_i. However, in_i chars moved from i-1 to i cost already counted as out_{i-1}. But if we choose in_i >0, the cost for i includes cnt[i]-k+in_i. Compare to choosing in_i=0 when cnt[i]>=k: cost cnt[i]-k. Since in_i cost at i-1 is paid regardless? Let's be careful: `in_i` is not a decision at i; it's `out_{i-1}`. If previous letter has surplus and we choose to move in_i to i, the cost `out_{i-1}` is counted at i-1. But previous letter's cost for surplus disposal is `cnt[i-1]-stay_{i-1}` regardless of out vs del? Actually for previous letter, cost = cnt[i-1]-stay_{i-1} + ins_{i-1}, independent of out_{i-1}. So moving in_i vs deleting at i-1 has same cost at i-1. Thus the total cost includes previous disposal cost whether in_i moved or deleted. Therefore when deciding at i, we should not add `in_i` again. The formula cost = abs(cnt[i] - (k - in_i)) counts current operations only, not the previous movement of in_i. But if in_i >0 and cnt[i] >= k-in_i, current cost = cnt[i]-k+in_i. This includes `in_i` as part of current cost? Let's see physical: If cnt[i] >= k-in_i, we keep k-in_i current chars at i, and the in_i chars also stay at i, total k. The remaining current chars cnt[i] - (k-in_i) = cnt[i]-k+in_i are either shifted/deleted. So current cost is cnt[i]-k+in_i. The in_i chars moved from previous cost was counted at previous. So total includes both. If we instead set in_i=0, current cost cnt[i]-k, previous surplus in_i deleted at previous cost same as moving? Previous disposal cost for those in_i chars is paid either way. Total with in_i=0 = previous disposal (including in_i) + cnt[i]-k. Total with in_i>0 = previous disposal (including in_i) + cnt[i]-k+in_i. So in_i>0 is worse by in_i. Thus optimal in_i=0 when cnt[i] >= k. This matches our recurrence. If cnt[i] < k-in_i, current cost = k-in_i-cnt[i]. Total with in_i = previous disposal + k-in_i-cnt[i]. If we instead set in_i=0, current cost = k-cnt[i], previous disposal for in_i chars paid anyway. Total = previous disposal + k-cnt[i]. Difference: with in_i>0 saves in_i in current insertion cost, but previous disposal same. So using in_i up to deficit is beneficial/equal? Compare total with in_i = prev_disposal + k-in_i-cnt; with in_i=0 = prev_disposal + k-cnt. With in_i>0 is smaller by in_i. Wait previous disposal includes cost for in_i chars either moved or deleted, same. So using them saves insertion. Thus optimal in_i = min(available, k-cnt[i]) if cnt[i]<k. This is our recurrence. Good.

This formal optimization shows:
- If cnt[i] >= k, set in_i=0, stay=k, cost cnt[i]-k, out_i can be cnt[i]-k (surplus) to help next.
- If cnt[i] < k, set in_i = min(avail, k-cnt[i]), stay=cnt[i], cost max(0,k-cnt[i]-in_i), out_i=0.
This is exactly our add formula. Great.

Now, what about if cnt[i] < k and avail > k-cnt[i], we set in_i = k-cnt[i], remaining avail deleted at previous. out_i=0. We could set in_i > deficit and out_i >0 (equal alternative), but optimization shows for fixed previous disposal cost, increasing in_i beyond deficit increases current cost by in_i - deficit? Let's see if in_i > k-cnt[i], then stay=k-in_i < cnt[i], current cost cnt[i]-stay = cnt[i]-(k-in_i)=cnt[i]-k+in_i. Total previous disposal + current. Compared to in_i = deficit, current cost0, total prev_disposal. Increasing in_i by x increases current cost by x, but previous disposal same. It may provide out_i? out_i = cnt[i]-stay = cnt[i]-k+in_i = x. So it shifts x current chars to next at cost x. If next needs them, saves insertion x. Net equal. So min unaffected. Thus setting in_i=deficit and out_i=0 is optimal for current prefix, and future can insert if needed. Good.

Thus recurrence is exact for min.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with previous availability `avail` when previous absent = cnt[i-1] but previous absent cost cnt[i-1] includes deletion of all previous chars. If we use in_i = min(avail, deficit), the remaining avail deleted at previous. Cost same. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with previous availability when previous present = max(0,cnt[i-1]-k). If previous present and cnt[i-1]>k, previous cost cnt[i-1]-k includes disposal of surplus. If we use in_i, remaining surplus deleted at previous. Cost same. If cnt[i-1]<=k, no surplus; previous may have received inflow and has no availability. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with previous absent and cnt[i-1] chars could have been shifted from i-2? We disallow because if i-1 absent, any chars from i-2 would have to move two steps to reach i-1 (or one step to i-1? Actually i-2 to i-1 is one step; if i-1 absent, they could move from i-2 to i-1 and then? They cannot stay because i-1 absent; they would need move to i, total two steps. We disallow. So previous absent availability only own cnt[i-1]. Good.

)=3.
i2 c0 prev5:

)=3.
ans3. Exact: b5 target b2,c2: shift2 b->c cost2, delete1 b cost1 total3. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the DP if a letter count is 0 and present, and previous absent count large used at it, and next present
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
class Solution:
    def makeStringGood(self, s: str) -> int:
        cnt = [0] * 26
        for ch in s:
            cnt[ord(ch) - 97] += 1

        max_cnt = max(cnt)
        ans = len(s)

        for k in range(1, max_cnt + 1):
            # dp0: current letter is absent
            # dp1: current letter is present with frequency k
            dp0 = cnt[0]
            dp1 = abs(cnt[0] - k)

            for i in range(1, 26):
                c = cnt[i]
                prev_c = cnt[i - 1]

                # Current letter absent: all its occurrences must be disposed.
                ndp0 = min(dp0, dp1) + c

                # Current letter present, previous letter absent.
                # Previous letter can provide up to prev_c characters.
                add0 = max(0, c - k) + max(0, k - c - prev_c)

                # Current letter present, previous letter present.
                # Previous letter can provide only its surplus.
                avail = max(0, prev_c - k)
                add1 = max(0, c - k) + max(0, k - c - avail)

                ndp1 = min(dp0 + add0, dp1 + add1)

                dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1

            ans = min(ans, min(dp0, dp1))

        return ans
```
